@@114560831 <h> Dubliners : 15 short stories about a football team and a city over 50 dramatic years <p> It 's half a century since Kevin Heffernan 's Dublin made their All-Ireland breakthrough . The capital would never be the same again , with Dublin footballers becoming box office . The city has changed dramatically since 1974 and social and football history are intertwined . <p> Jimmy Keaveney had retired from intercounty football after growing sick of a run of eight seasons in a row where the Dubs did n't even reach a Leinster final . <p> Dublin had beaten Wexford by 12 points but , as he sat stewing in the car , Heffernan was n't happy . He felt his team had a freetaking issue that would cost them down the road . That 's when a six-year-old made one of the most important interventions in Dublin football history . <p> " I loved Jimmy and I said something about him never missing frees for Vincent 's . My mother has an ongoing argument about it with me , she always maintains it was her that said it , ' ' said Jennings . <p> The seed was planted in Heffo 's mind . By the end of that summer , Dublin were All-Ireland champions , with the returning Keaveney playing a pivotal role . <p> His Indian summer would see him win three All-Irelands , three All Stars and six Leinsters . <p> " Every time my father would be in the Vincent 's clubhouse and he 'd spot Jimmy coming in , he 'd call over ' you have to decorate the bar , you still owe me ' , ' ' said Jennings . <p> " I 've never talked in public about this before , but it keeps coming up . It 's mentioned in a few books , it was in the Irish Times again there a few months ago . ' ' <p> Nearly two decades on , Jennings was given his own shot at a place in the Dublin team , getting the call from Paddy Cullen early in 1992 . <p> " I was brought into the panel and I felt like an imposter . I did n't feel comfortable at all there . I did n't feel like I was good enough , ' ' he said . <p> " I was playing very good football with Vincent 's but , when I went training there , I got a shock . It was a different level of pace . I could n't enjoy it . <p> " In those days there were 30 on a panel . If I was going to make that , I was going to be number 28 or 29 . I was never going to be 22 . <p> " When we were doing sprint drills , I had to concentrate on every sprint , not to be last each time . Some of the players were just sailing through it . ' ' <p> Jennings has a killer line that he used to throw around . A claim that he spent 20 years trying to get on the Dublin panel , and six months trying to get off it . <p> " I probably had a few drinks on me when I 'd say that . To be honest , I could n't wait to get out of there . I liked Paddy Cullen . It was nothing to do with him or the players . I just was n't comfortable , ' ' he said. ; <p> " I actually dropped myself off the panel . Never told anyone I was going , just stopped going to training . " <p> Jennings is coaching now , concentrating on ladies football with Kilmacud Crokes , getting involved when his daughter , Mia , started playing with them . <p> He works closely with Paddy O'Donoghue , who he used to play with in Trinity . O'Donoghue was one of Pat Gilroy 's selectors when Dublin won their breakthrough All-Ireland in the modern era in 2011 . <p> " People would say to me that my father would be turning in his grave because I went to Crokes , no he would n't , ' ' said Jennings . <p> " I remember him specifically saying to me to bring the kids to where their freinds are playing . <p> " I actually had Darragh over in the mini-leagues in Vincent 's for a while but , once your kids are involved in a club , you row in . I did feel a bit awkward when we played Vincent 's sometimes , but that 's just me . " <h> AN ENCOUNTER <p> KEVIN McMANAMON is one of the children of the revolution . His goal against Kerry in the 2011 All-Ireland final released so much tension in the Dublin crowd that the stands in Croke Park shook . <p> It was the day that McManamon became the first St Jude 's player to win Sam Maguire . When Sean Doherty lifted Sam above his head after the 1974 final , there was nobody from Jude 's in Croke Park . That 's because the club did n't exist . <p> It was founded four years later , with the founders surfing on the blue wave released by Heffo and his men . <p> " According to my parents , it was all farmland back then in Templeogue . Willington was just being built and there was nowhere to play , ' ' said McManamon . <p> " The Bishop Galvin National School got going and it all came from there . My father would always talk about the ' 70s team and David Hickey , in particular . <p> " We had a VHS of the Dubs at home and myself and my brother would watch it in the morning before going to watch Dublin play . <p> " When I was brought into the panel , Hickey was there with Pat Gilroy . He knew what it meant to be a Dublin player , to be picked to wear the shirt . Hickey had very much lived it . ' ' <p> The belief in a Dublin Way went as deep with Jim Gavin as with Gilroy . <p> A few days after his initial press conference as manager , he met in a more informal setting with reporters to discuss their requirements . <p> During that meeting , Gavin showed the media video clips of some of Dublin 's most glorious days . He did the same when first meeting with the players . <p> Dublin 's past - as well as its present and future - mattered greatly to Gavin . <p> " He 'd show us clips of games from different years . I remember he had the relatives of the first Dublin All-Ireland winning team from 1891 come to meet us , ' ' said McManamon . <p> " Anton O'Toole came in to talk to us . There was one season where there was a lot of Heffo paraphernalia around the place . ' ' <p> It was n't an easy summer for McManamon as his beloved father , Maxie , passed away in June . <p> A short break in Paris for the Olympics did him the power of good , teaming up with Michael Darragh Macauley to cheer Kellie Harrington to boxing gold . <p> There was a ritual that Kevin and Maxie would have on the day after Dublin won All-Irelands . <p> Both were musicians as well as football men , and they 'd meet up in the Palace Bar on Fleet Street for a sing-song . <p> Mostly Dublin songs , and McManamon has been struck by an upsurge in Dub pride - from Harrington in the ring to his beloved musical idol Damien Dempsey to actor Barry Keoghan . <p> And a recent encounter showed him that heroes come in all forms . <p> " Maybe there was a stigma with the accent before but there are so many real role models in Dublin . Just look at the Stardust relatives , ' ' he said . <p> " Growing up in Templeogue , we had a neutral enough accent but I love that really strong Dublin lilt . I got involved in a men 's group recently and you 'd hear the most harrowing stories there . <p> " Stories of addiction and mad lives . But these men are helping to carry other men out of the ashes . It 's beautiful to see . <p> " These are strong men who know how to put on the armour and be tough as nails but they know how to take it off too . " <p> After McManamon 's goal , and Stephen Cluxton 's free-kick , and Bryan Cullen saying ' see yiz in Coppers ' , they were true to their captain 's word . <p> Once the last of the regular punters were ushered out the door at 3am , the lock-in started . <p> Damien Dempsey got his guitar out and they sang their hearts out until dawn . <p> " I ca n't speak highly enough of Damo . He 's just so unapologetically himself . He has a bit of everything in him . Reggae , sean-n ? s , songs from his grandparents ' day . He sings them in his own way and that makes him unique , ' ' said McManamon . <p> " I think it 's his authenticity that 's appealing to people . It 's like he 's saying ' this is what I do , this is my upbringing ' . He wears a heart on his sleeve , a lot of people in Dublin could learn from Damo . " <p> McManamon is part of the 2011 Old Lads WhatsApp group now . He 's been outside the intercounty bubble for three years . <p> With that comes a bit of perspective , a realisation just what a glorious era meant to the people of his city . <p> " We were all doing stuff that we would have done anyway . I 'd have gone to training , the gym , training camps because I loved it , ' ' he said . <p> " It cost me nothing but what it gave to others ... even with people going through bad times , it gave them a bit of hope . Something to chase , something to follow . <p> " Even with me , following the team has given me a bit of meaning . For us , I think there was something fateful about Jim coming along at just the right time . <p> " I was over at the Tokyo Olynpics working as a sports pyscholgogist with the Irish boxers . Do you know Mick he Busker ? You often see him on Henry Street . <p> " The night before Kellie Harrington 's final , Mick sang a song he 'd written about her outsider her gaff . We were looking at a video of it over in Tokyo and it was very emotional . <p> " There may be something to the idea that there 's an upsurge in Dublin pride , between people like Kellie , different bands and so on . And the football team too . <p> " I did n't really get the impact that our success had on people until I stepped away . It was like gold for people . " <h> ARABY <p> THE DUBLIN footballers did n't manage it in 2024 , but the Leinster rugby team did . <p> When they bowed out of the Championship to Galway in the All-Ireland quarter-finals , they did so in front of their biggest crowd of the year - but one that was below 50,000 . <p> Leinster , though , sold out Croke Park in May for their clash with Northampton Saints . Over 80,000 tickets were snapped up in just 36 hours . <p> It 's a reminder of how the sporting landscape in the capital is an ever changing one . <p> Back in 1995 , Dublin - as reigning All-Ireland champions - would have laughed off any threat from rugby . <p> The Heineken Cup was launched that summer and , in its first few years , Leinster crowds in Donnybrook were numbered in the hundreds rather than the thousands . <p> But momentum began to build . Donnybrook became too small , Leinster moved to the RDS . Season tickets started to fly off the shelves . <p> A few weeks after their 2011 All-Ireland win , the Dublin County Board launched their ' Blue Wave ' action plan for the future . <p> It was clear that they now viewed Leinster as a threat , judging from this passage . <p> " The Blue Jersey is an unique , inclusive brand , uniting Dublin 's dense expanse , blurring the difference in class and possession which became so pointedly manifest during the delusional days of the Celtic Tiger . <p> " It is an extraordinarily effective promotional tool , enshrined in the anthem of the county 's often maligned but fiercely loyal supporters . <p> " We ca n't copyright a colour but the subliminal exploitation of Dublin 's unique sporting hue by our competitors has not gone unnoticed . " <p> Leinster were n't mentioned by name but it 's clear that the Dubs felt their ' Blue Jersey ' brand was being usurped . <p> There was a time when Leinster 's home in the RDS was known in the capital as Araby . It 's due to the Araby Bazaar that took place there in 1894 . <p> It was a phenomenal success . At a time when Dublin had a population just below 350,000 , over 90,000 visited Araby . <p> Araby is a movable feast now . The Leinster rugby team are happy to book the Aviva or Croke Park , confident that tickets will fly . <p> That they are often chasing the same supporters - and recruits - as the Dubs is something that former GAA director general P ? raic Duffy once acknowledged . <p> " We have become a more middle class game in Dublin . To be fair , the Dublin County Board recognise that , ' ' he said . <p> " But it is a major challenge because you have to commit so many resources and send in so many volunteers . The GAA asks so much of its clubs that it probably puts people off . <p> " In working class Dublin , soccer is by far the biggest sport . We have some very good clubs in working class areas but overall participation and interest in our games is very , very low . <p> " In vast tracts of urban Ireland there is very little interest in our affairs . That 's the reality . " <p> Heffo 's Dubs had high achievers in everything from medicine to business on board , but the squad was backboned by players from the traditional working-class strongholds of Sheriff Street , Marino , Fairview , Ballybough , Drimnagh , Finglas , Whitehall , Glasnevin and the South Circular Road . <p> In the modern era , though , Dublin have looked to players from Dalkey , Castleknock , Malahide , Lucan , Skerries , Portmarnock , Stillorgan , Blackrock , Rathfarnham and Knocklyon . The comfortable suburbs of the modern , sprawling city . <p> According to the economist , David McWilliams , a societal shift also impacted on the sporting landscape . <p> " The most successful people in Ireland since the introduction of free education in the 1960s have been the sons and daughters of small farmers , ' ' he said . <p> " Their kids become school-teachers , and their kids become doctors and lawyers . They live in south Dublin and send their kids to private schools , but retain the love of GAA . <p> " That 's the main reason why GAA has gone massively up the socio-economic bracket in south Dublin . <p> " I 've heard what I would call the GAA professional fathers being quite aggressively anti the rugby culture of south Dublin . <p> " And yet they 're very happy to send their kids to the private schools that propagate the rugby culture . But they ensure that their kids play GAA outside of school . " <p> A few years ago , there were more Blackrock College old boys playing for the Dubs than for the Leinster rugby team . <p> It 's not that long since that would have been unthinkable . <p> McWilliams feels the GAA have an advantage that is often overlooked . <p> " You 've also got to understand that the GAA is run very professionally by professional people , ' ' he said . <p> " You can be partner in a law firm or PWC by day , and be a volunteer in the local GAA club by night . ' ' <h> EVELINE <p> OF ALL the men of 1974 , none has been more influential than David Hickey . So many of those who 've played in Dublin have got involved in social and community causes . Political ones too . <p> There is a ' Dublin Footballers Support Palestine ' group . Current players like Jack McCaffrey and Michael Fitzsimons have marched with them . <p> Plenty of retirees from John McCarthy to Barney Rock to Michael Darragh Macauley have done so too . <p> But there is one constant : Hickey . When he answers the call , he 's on his way to a protest outside the Israeli embassy . Rarely a week goes by without him being there . <p> When the ' 74 men were honoured as the silver jubilee team before the 1999 All-Ireland final , Hickey had three words etched on the back of his white shirt - End Cuban Blockade . <p> Hickey has always stood up and spoken out . To him , the highlight of the summer was n't anything that happened on a football field . Rather , it was the stance taken by Trinity College students that led eventually to the university cutting ties with Israeli companies . <p> " It was very heartening to see it . The young people of today breeze through life , in some sense . There is no sense of protest . This is the first time that it 's happened in a very long time , ' ' siad Hickey . <p> " I am so gratified to see it happen in Trinity , of all places , but there 's been nothing done from pillars of our so-called society . <p> There is n't a word from the Vatican about it . I played for the Dublin footballers and the Leinster rugby team . There were Palestine flags at Dublin games . They have been at all sorts of GAA and League of Ireland matches . <p> " Leinster filled Croke Park and there was n't a Palestine flag to be seen . Those people are unaware and do n't care . ' ' <p> Macauley has something of Hickey in him . The two men are fond of each other , and Hickey laughs when he remembers a recent encounter with the Ballyboden man . <p> " I bumped into Mick and told him that I liked his new trousers . He was puzzled , asking how I knew they were new . The bloody price tag was still on them ! It could only happen to Mick ! ' ' <p> When Hickey made his debut at just 17 in 1969 , it was only six years since the Dubs had been All-Ireland champions.b But they 'd fallen far and fallen fast . <p> Hickey 's first four Championships with Dublin stretched to just four games . All four of those games - against Laois , Kildare , Longford and Louth - ended in defeat . <p> In 1973 , he decided to give rugby a go , having tried it while studying medicine in UCD . <p> But Kevin Heffernan , who carried the weightiest of reputations from his playing days , had taken over as Dublin manager that winter and put a call in to Hickey . <p> The Leinster opener against Wexford was fixed for May 19 . It would n't go ahead . <p> That was due to the events of two days earlier . That Friday was one of the darkest days in Ireland 's modern history . <p> At rush hour in Dublin , three bombs went off on Talbot Street , Parnell Street and South Leinster Street . <p> The death toll in Dublin was initially 23 , with another three people dying from their injuries in the weeks afterwards . An unborn baby was lost in Dublin too . <p> Just 90 minutes after the Dublin bombings , another car bomb went off in Monaghan town . The death toll was seven . All of the bombs were planted by the loyalist UVF . <p> It was the bloodiest day in the entire history of the Troubles . <p> Ireland was in shock . There was no way that Dublin 's game could go ahead so it was postponed for a week . <p> When Hickey thinks of that bleak day , he remembers panic . <p> " My brother was studying in Belvedere at the time and I was working in Vincent 's as a final year medical student , ' ' he said . <p> " I remember panicking and getting to a phone to ring home because the kids from Belvedere would go down Talbot Street to get to Connolly Station . I was delighted to hear that he had got home earlier than normal . <p> " The bombings had a huge impact on everyone in the city . It was something the players talked about a lot . ' ' <p> A city in mourning would get something of a lift from an unexpected All-Ireland , but Hickey remembers a sense of anti-climax after the final win over Galway . <p> " The big one was beating Cork in the All-Ireland semi-final . It was the first time I felt this crescendo of noise from Dublin supporters . I think it was one of our best ever performances , but I 'm not sure if there 's any footage of it . <p> " The Horse Show was on the same day and RTE had only one outside broadcast unit so that was sent to cover Harvey Smith and Lady Montgomery jumping over fences . <p> " But the final ... it was strange , the weather was terrible and the evening afterwards summed up where we stood . Either no hotel in Dublin would take our booking or the County Board would n't pay what they wanted , but we ended up going to a hotel in Kildare . A pissing wet , miserable night . Did n't enjoy it at all . " <p> Hickey 's wandering spirit took him to France for a couple of years . He played rugby for La Rochelle and it was in France that he came across the meaning of the name ' Eveline ' . Source of life . <p> Hickey 's career as a transplant surgeon was all about the gift of life . He is engaged so much by Gaza because of the wanton destruction of life there . <p> " In transplant surgery , there 's both an immense justice and injustice . In a family 's absolute , ultimate torture , the gift of life is passed on , ' ' he said . <p> " That 's the humanity I was saturated in for 40 years , it had to rub off in some way . We have to remember where we came from and not be cruel to people . <p> " Remember the old signs - ' no blacks , no dogs , no Irish ' . We 'd have no health service without the people coming in here . They 're keeping Ireland afloat . " <h> AFTER THE RACE <p> KIERAN DUFF grew up just yards away from Swords Celtic 's ground and soccer was his game . In thrall to the Holy Trinity of Best , Law and Charlton , Gaelic football was an after-thought . <p> That was the way of things in Dublin of the early 1970s , before Kevin Heffernan tore up the script 50 years ago . Duff was 13 , an impressionable age , and he would never be the same again . <p> " When Dublin won in ' 74 , I was just ... ' wow , this is the game I want to play ' , ' ' recalled Duff . <p> " So I concentrated on the Gaelic from then . I would have played just a few games with the school team and with Fingallians but Dublin winning the All-Ireland really caught my imagination . <p> " Big guys playing at 100 miles an hour ... it made me want to play for Dublin . " <p> Duff progressed to the Dublin minors , playing in the 1978 and 1979 All-Ireland finals , before Heffo called him into the senior squad . <p> A couple of lean years then , bang , 1983 . The best of times , the worst of times . <p> Duff won his only All-Ireland medal , but he was one of three Dublin players sent off in the final win over Galway . <p> He lashed out with a boot at Pat O'Neill and was hit with a 12 month ban . <p> Duff felt that he got a reputation from then on which did him no favours . <p> " I never , ever made a big deal out of it at the time . What happens on the field happens and you do n't whinge and cry after it , ' ' he said . <p> " I played for nearly another 10 years after that and never got sent off for Dublin again . <p> " Fellas pulled , dragged , tried to intimidate me , did everything to me off the ball . <p> " When it was my free , refs would n't give it to me . If I touched a fella , they 'd give a free against me . <p> " That certainly happened for a good few years afterwards . " <p> Many in the capital felt Dublin got a raw deal from the authorities at the time , and Duff agrees . <p> " The GAA wanted Dublin there but they did n't want Dublin to be successful , ' ' he said . <p> " You go back to years previous . Jimmy Keaveney got sent off in a Leinster final and got suspended for three months and missed an All-Ireland final . That never happened to anyone before . <p> " Tony Hanahoe criticised the referee , Seamus Aldridge , in another year . He got suspended . <p> " Kevin Heffernan got suspended in ' 83 for three months for going on to the field . Mattie McDonagh was on the field more than anyone else and there was no suspension handed out . The GAA always seemed to hammer Dublin when something happened . " <p> In January , 1986 , Heffernan decided his race was run . The 1984 and 1985 All-Ireland finals had been lost to Kerry . Duff was a month away from his 25th birthday when Heffo stepped down as manager . He did n't know that the hungry years were back . Brian Mullins , Robbie Kelleher and Sean Doherty took the team for the 1986 season , then Gerry McCaul took over . <p> " We beat Kerry in the League final in ' 87 and I got a goal . I caught the ball on the run and stuck it in the bottom corner , ' ' said Duff . <p> " I remember thinking ' Jaysus , how did that go in ? ' because I did n't think I 'd struck it that well . <p> " There were experienced lads there and good young fellas had come in . <p> " When you beat Kerry ... you just think ' this team is on the rise again ' . But , unfortunately , we hit Meath at their best . <p> " We were unlucky in those years that a very good Meath team came along and there was often a controversial score that decided those matches . <p> " We 'd have felt that we were going to drive on but Meath put us back in our place . " <p> Duff 's inter-county career ended when Paddy Cullen took over as Dublin manager . <p> And many felt he was let go too soon , and that he could have made the difference in the 1992 All-Ireland final defeat to Donegal . <p> " Go back to the four games against Meath in ' 91 . I came on as a sub in the first one , started the second and did n't get a look-in in the other two . <p> " I was just 30 but I was thinking ' I have n't a future with these guys ' <p> " They called me back for the League , played the games before Christmas , did well , but then I was sub , sub , sub and was dropped off the bench altogether for the League semi-final with Tyrone . <p> " My club form was good , my training was good ... by this stage , I was fuming because my life was playing for Dublin . <p> " I was thinking ' fuck them , I 'm not going down there Thursday night ' . I did go down in a bit of a thick mood and played an A v B game . <p> " What used to happen was that , if you won a ball against a first 15 player , a free would be given against you . <p> " This happened to me a few times . That really pissed me off . At the end , I said to John O'Leary ' that 's it , they do n't have the fucking decency to tell me where I stand and then blow me up every time I win a ball ' . <p> " Tyrone beat us on the Sunday . I went for a few pints after , met Vinnie Murphy and Eamon Heery in Meagher 's and went ' look it , I 'm finished here ' . That was it . " <h> TWO GALLANTS <p> THERE was a time when the manager of the Castleknock Under-14s rubbed shoulders with Premier League stars . The footage is up there on YouTube . There he is , showing the basics of Gaelic football to Ashley Young , Jermain Defoe and Roman Pavlyuchenko . <p> All four men were adidas clients and had been brought to a pitch in west London for a photo shoot in 2009 . <p> That 's the kind of stature the Castleknock Under-14 manager used to have though , at the time , there were mutterings that it was undeserved . <p> After all , Alan Brogan was in his eighth season with Dublin and had n't even played in an All-Ireland final , let alone won one . <p> That was a decade when a TV pundit dismissed the Dubs as ' ' Hollywood footballers ' ' . <p> But Brogan was an All-Ireland winner in his 10th season . He 'd finish that 2011 as Footballer of the Year too , succeeding his brother , Bernard . <p> Alan would walk away in 2015 as a three time All-Ireland winner , a three time All Star . <p> Bernard kept going until 2019 , finishing with seven All-Irelands and four All Stars . <p> They both lived up to the name of the father - Bernard Sr was one of Heffo 's main men in the 1970s . <p> " We were very aware of what my father did but there was no pressure . When I was growing up , my uncle Jimmy was a selector with Dublin , ' ' said Alan . <p> " We 'd go to a lot of games all over the place with Jimmy so I was very exposed to the Dublin scene and it kind of helped to make it normal . " <p> " I did n't know if I was going to play for Dublin but it was n't something that felt like it was a million miles away . " <p> Alan was a Dublin regular from 20 , Bernard took a tougher route . <p> " It took me until I was 24 to get that jersey . You need to have self-confidence without being arrogant , ' ' he said . <p> " If you do n't think you 're good enough , you 're wasting your time . I 'm an ultra competitive guy . I was determined to get that shirt . " <p> There is a strong case to be made for Bernard being Dublin 's greatest ever forward . <p> In so many of the big games under Jim Gavin , he made all the difference . <p> It says a lot too that , when he was named Footballer of the Year in 2010 , it was widely accepted that he was a deserved winner , even though Dublin lost in the All-Ireland semi-finals to Cork . <p> Directly opposite the Brogan family home in Castleknock is acres of parkland and Bernard admits to knowing every blade of grass . <p> " We 'd be out there every chance we 'd get . Every day after school and morning till night during the holidays , ' ' he said . <p> " There were goalposts there and we 'd play more soccer than Gaelic - headers and volleys and stuff like that . <p> " We were always pretending to be Brazil and the Ronaldos and those type of players . " <p> Alan and Bernard have their own kids now , chasing their own dreams in the park . <p> Plenty of sportspeople struggle with the afterlife , finding it hard to let go . Bernard 's first year outside of the Dublin bubble was during lockdown and that might have helped his transition . <p> " It was a big part of my identity for so long , playing for Dublin . A lot of those guys are good friends of mine , I 'd be close to them , but I did n't have that pain of nostalgia watching them from the stands . <p> " Instead , because of Covid , I was watching games on TV and it did n't feel real . You feel disconnected . <p> " It was strange , that first year . I actually had dreams about playing for Dublin , kicking points in my sleep . I 'm not joking , those thoughts are going through my head when I 'm asleep . It was just a massive part of my life for a long time . ' ' <p> For Alan , the biggest buzz he gets is seeing what the Dubs mean to the next generation . <p> " I am a Dublin fan but the whole thing is more about my own kids wanting to go and watch games , ' ' he said . <p> " To be honest , I 'm like any supporter now , I do n't know when the matches are going to be on . It has lost a little bit of razzmatazz over the last couple years but it 's about my kids now . <p> " My oldest boy , Jamie , goes along to the matches on his own and the other two boys are mad for it . <p> " I have a love for Dublin GAA and I am very much there as a supporter . Always will be . " <h> THE BOARDING HOUSE <p> THERE were days together that stood out . Like a visit to Portmarnock Beach one sunny afternoon . <p> The Ireland soccer team were staying in a nearby hotel and Michael Murphy and Paul Flynn bumped into Seamus Coleman and James McCarthy . Murphy knew Coleman from Donegal GAA circles so they all shot the breeze . <p> There was another day when Murphy and Aidan Walsh headed to Carton House to watch the Lions train , and they managed to have a brief chat with Sam Warburton . <p> Murphy , Walsh and Flynn shared accommodation in DCU and there were plenty of evenings when they gave voice to their dreams . <p> Little did they know the symmetry of them being realised . Walsh won an All-Ireland with Cork in 2010 , Flynn got his hands on it with Dublin a year later , and Murphy led Donegal to the summit as captain in 2012 . <p> Walsh and Murphy would n't get back up there , but Flynn was about to reap a golden harvest , There were so many days Paul Flynn lost himself in a rhapsody of blue . <p> When you walk away with six All-Irelands and four All Stars , then it 's clear that you had plenty of good ones . <p> He was one of the DCU crew , and the university played a massive role - on and off the pitch . Mickey Whelan and Niall Moyna played pivotal roles on Pat Gilroy 's management team and both worked for DCU . <p> When Kevin Heffernan played and managed , DCU did n't even exist . Unusually for the time , he went to Trinity College and the Kerry poet Brendan Kennelly was a teammate on their football team . <p> And Kennelly loved to tell the story of the 1951 Duke Cup final when UCD led Trinity by 14 points at half-time . <p> Trinity needed a strong second half and won a penalty almost from the throw-in . Heffo blasted it wide . <p> " Having missed the penalty , the man went mad , and inside 10 minutes , he had the ball three times in the UCD net , and then added a several points , ' ' said Kennelly . <p> " Heffo was a great full-forward because he was an efficient and stylish savage of a player , who was at his best when he was slightly humiliated . <p> " If he had scored the penalty , we would have lost . He might have relaxed . He might have lost his demon energy . But he did n't , and we won , because he was suddenly humiliated into greatness . " <p> Heffo 's captain in the ' 70s , Tony Hanahoe , was another Trinity man , as was Pat Gilroy , but it was on Gilroy 's watch that the DCU connection became paramount . <p> Former Taoiseach Bertie Ahern , though , remembers days in DCU before Gilroy , days that played a significant role in the transformation of Dublin GAA . <p> There was a time when he was very close to the Dublin football set-up . For much of the nineties and noughties , Ahern was a regular at their post-match meals . <p> He has often been criticised for being calculated in building up his image as a quintessential Dub for political purposes , something that nagged at Ahern . <p> " I used to get stuff thrown at me , and the only people that threw it were those who probably only turn up for All-Ireland semi-finals and finals , ' ' he said . <p> " You know the ' rent-a-crowd ' Dublin supporters . I 'd have been going to club matches . Parnell Park , O'Toole Park , everywhere from the time I was a kid with my father and younger brother . <p> " I always went back with the team in Pillar Caffrey 's time and Tommy Lyons ' time . I 'd join them for dinner , and the cup of tea and the few drinks . It was in Jury 's , originally , during the 1990s and then to DCU later on . " <p> There would often be talk of serious work being needed at grassroots level . It would cost money to put full time coaches into every club and school , Ahern delivered a large amount of it - something that has rankled with plenty in other counties . <p> " Yeah , I was involved in giving the financial aid but Dublin is an enormous place and needed it , ' ' he said . <p> " The notion that everyone in Dublin is from Dublin or plays Gaelic is just nonsense . <p> " A huge proportion of the population is in Dublin and you need money to fund the games here . <p> " I remember club championship games back in the 1970s when it 'd be 13 against 12 because of a lack of players in Dublin . <p> " That 's how bleak it was before the Heffo days . Thankfully , it 's now in a very different place . ' ' <p> Ahern was around for plenty of dark days so the golden era was hard to get used to . <p> " A lot of younger supporters do n't remember the heartache of the period between 1995 and 2011 , ' ' he said . <p> " Dublin were losing to Kerry , to Down , to Mayo , to Derry , to Tyrone , to Donegal , to Laois , to Kildare ... <p> " Now people are giving out about Dublin being dominant and the money and everything . <p> " But Kerry were winning three or four All-Irelands every decade and no-one was getting excited over that . <p> " I do n't know why people get excited when we have one good decade . " <h> A LITTLE CLOUD <p> THE sun had risen , but the party had n't stopped . It was Monday , June 13 , 1988 and a tiny bar in the mountains of the Black Forest had become an Irish colony . <p> Hours earlier , Ray Houghton had scored the goal that he was born to score - taking down England in Ireland 's European Championship opener . <p> Houghton , his Ireland teammates , Jack Charlton and the other coaches , and a few dozen fans had squeezed into the Brew Haus . Kevin Moran made sure he got the best table . Bit of elbow room , fresh air , short hop to the bar . That was Moran 's way . <p> And , as the ground outside started to warm in the early morning June sun , he shook his head and took on a wistful air . <p> " I 'd give anything to be in Ballybunion now . " <p> Before wearing the green of Ireland , Moran had shone in the sky-blue of Dublin . <p> For Dubs of that era , thoughts of Kerry were never far from their minds . Even on the greatest of soccer days . <p> Vinnie Murphy is unique in that he wore both the Dublin and Kerry colours . <p> Football for Dublin , hurling for Kerry - but he also lined out in a trial game for the Kerry footballers under P ? id ? ? S ? . <p> It should never have happened . In another era , Murphy would have been weighed down with Leinster and All-Ireland medals . <p> It started for him later in that same summer of ' 88 . Dublin manager Gerry McCaul took a long look at the big 18-year-old from Donaghmede and made the call . <p> Vinnie Murphy looked ready . Sure it would be the ultimate baptism of fire , but McCaul figured he was Dublin 's best bet of getting any change out of feared Meath full-back Mick Lyons . <p> Dublin leaked two goals early on and then had Dave Synott sent off and Charlie Redmond missed a late penalty . They still only lost by two points to a Dublin team that would go on to retain the All-Ireland . <p> It was the story of Dublin so often in the post Heffo era . Nursing regrets and brooding over ' if onlys ' . <p> Murphy has his own story of how he managed to get past all of that . <p> " When we won the All-Ireland in 1995 , I was n't making the team but I was training hard and going well and hopeful of getting a good bit of time off the b bench against Tyrone in the final , ' ' he said . <p> " But the clock was ticking down and nothing was happening with Pat O'Neill . I was getting frantic but , eventually , he gave me the nod . <p> " I ran on and was desperate to get involved . There 's a photograph that often does the rounds of Jim Gavin celebrating at the final whistle on his knees . <p> " When that snap was taken , I was in mid air ready to catch the ball . Paddy Russell blew the whistle before it came into my hands . <p> " I got 70 seconds on the pitch and my only touch was after the final whistle . For years , that got to me . <p> " I did n't feel I 'd really won an All-Ireland . I felt that I did n't deserve the medal . It might sound like a joke , but I had it thrown in a sock drawer for a long time . " <p> Murphy does n't feel like that anymore . What changed his mind was a holiday to Donegal over a decade ago . <p> He was driving around the county and could n't help but notice the Donegal flags everywhere . <p> He 'd go into shops and bars and there 'd be photos of the Donegal team and of Jim McGuinness on the walls . <p> " Donegal had n't even had a good Championship that year but I was blown away by the pride in McGuinness and the team , ' ' said Murphy . <p> " Now Donegal have only ever won two All-Irelands so it 's obviously a huge deal when they do win , but it should be no different elsewhere . <p> " I was thinking of really talented players that I played with - like Ciaran Whelan , Eamon Heery and Paddy Christie - that do n't have an All-Ireland medal . <p> " I was feeling sorry for myself because of not getting a touch in a final , but I have a medal . I realised that I was blessed . <p> " The medal came out of the sock drawer and it has pride of place in the house now . <p> " That trip to Donegal made me cop on how big a deal winning an All-Ireland really is . ' ' <p> Murphy was an All Star in 1992 and , if Dublin had n't imploded against Donegal in the All-Ireland final , he 'd have been in the running for Footballer of the Year . <p> That was one of the most frustrating eras of all for Dublin . Losing after four games to Meath in 1991 , final defeats to Donegal and Down in ' 92 and ' 94 , a semi-final loss to Derry in ' 93 . <p> Everything would be blamed - from hype to referees . Murphy has given the matter plenty of thought . He feels the issues were within the camp . <p> I always feel that the best team comes out on top . The teams that beat us deserved to beat us , ' ' he said . <p> " With that Dublin team of the 1990s , there was often that attitude along the lines of ' it 's someone else 's fault ' or ' it was like that when I got here ' . <p> " We bought into this notion that we had the best footballing team , and just fell short due to outside factors . It was nonsense . " <h> COUNTERPARTS <p> PADDY O'Donoghue did n't play many games in Croke Park , but a few stand out . Top of the list is the 1995 All-Ireland club final against Bellaghy . O'Donoghue was man of the match as Kilmacud Crokes took the title . <p> The first time is always special too , and O'Donoghue 's Croker debut was in 1981 . An Under-14 Leinster final for Dublin against Offaly which was played before the minor and senior deciders . <p> But you 'd forgive him if , over the next while , he harps on about his second game in Croke Park . Oasismania is all the rage and the reformed Manchester band 's dates on Jones 's Road next year will be a sort of homecoming for Noel Gallagher . <p> In 1983 , he played for Oisin 's in a challenge match at Croke Park . Up against them were Crokes , and O'Donoghue was part of their line-up . <p> " Someone in Oisin 's had Crokes connections and that 's how the game was arranged , ' ' said O'Donoghue . <p> " It was a home and away arrangement . They came here first and then we went over to Manchester to play them . <p> " We were just 16 and we got some education in life in the Irish Club over there ! ' ' <p> Crokes are now one of the biggest and most successful clubs in Ireland but , when Heffo 's Army were on the march , it was very different . <p> The southside of Dublin was largely seen as a GAA wasteland . Up until the late 1980s , the county title was effectively a northside preserve . <p> " I did the Leaving in 1985 and played in the senior championship the following year , ' ' said O'Donoghue . <p> " In my first six years of playing senior , we won two championship matches . Two . <p> " One thing that really helped us was the free from the hand , when that came in . We had athletes rather than big strong men who might have been working in manual labour . <p> " They 'd knock lumps out of us coming up through the ranks . The free out of the hand sped up the game and meant there was less contact , it really benefited us . " <p> The catchment area of Crokes takes in some of the most well heeled suburbs in Dublin . <p> GAA has become fashionable , to an extent , but some old prejudices still linger . <p> " There is a south county Dublin snobbery because you are culchies playing Gaelic football , ' ' said O'Donoghue . <p> " South county Dublin was and still is a rugby heartland . One of our biggest issues is that we lose players to Blackrock , St. Michael 's , Leinster and Ireland . <p> " I 'd say most of the Leinster players from South County Dublin have probably played for Kilmacud Crokes . I even saw Leo Cullen play an Under-16 championship game for us . <p> " He had no football but he was 6 ' 6 or so and a beanpole , so the ball was pumped into him at full-forward . " <p> After his own playing days ended , O'Donoghue immersed himself in coaching . <p> He 'd had a couple of short spells on the Dublin panel but did n't have a high profile . <p> So when he got a call from the newly appointed Pat Gilroy asking him to come on board as a selector , he was surprised . <p> To be there for the breakthrough in 2011 ... it was beyond words . O'Donoghue is clear-eyed in what Dublin did to make themselves a genuine force . <p> " I think one thing was that humility was brought into the group . Going back 20 years , there was definitely a perception of Dublin enjoying success and the attractions and trimmings that came with it , ' ' he said . <p> " In the Celtic Tiger days , there was a Dublin player at every press conference for anything . I think just grounding the group into practicalities and the importance of hard work was huge . <p> " If you go back to the first couple of years we were there , it was n't easy . There were ' Gilroy Out ' signs on the Hill . You can see them on clips on YouTube . <p> " There was a game against Wexford that went to extra-time . I remember walking across the pitch and I could hear the boos coming from the stand . <p> " I remember a qualifier against Tipperary and Rory O'Carroll was getting dog 's abuse from the Hill . <p> " The hammering we got from Meath in 2010 was definitely a wake up call . Some of the back door matches that year made us more humble . We started to get some momentum and led Cork - the eventual champions - all through the semi-final until injury time . " <p> When Gilroy took over the Dublin hurlers , he again brought O'Donoghue with him . <p> He enjoyed that experience but he is happy to be back coaching with his first love . <p> Crokes are a force at every level now but few realise how hard it is to keep the show on the road . <p> " We have three playing pitches of our own and we are using county council pitches for the kids . We have four minor girls teams , three minor boys teams , three in football and three in hurling and so on . The number of teams is incredible , ' ' he said <p> " I do n't know how we do it but it does get done and it does require all four codes to help each other out . We played a few matches recently at <p> 8.15pm because a team needs to train from 7pm to 8pm . We could warm up on the side of the pitch . <p> " Everyone realises that we do n't have enough green grass or even plastic grass . We have to juggle and cooperate . It 's mind-boggling . " <p> Maybe Noel Gallagher went down the easier road ... <h> CLAY <p> DAVID Walsh has held on to a copy of the magazine . He glances at the cover photograph of Kevin Heffernan taken by Billy Stickland . <p> It shows Heffernan with a faraway look in his eyes , sitting on a Parnell Park dressingroom bench with Dublin shirts hanging on pegs around him . <p> " I remember it was my idea that Billy should take the pics . Vincent Browne was editor of Magill . I went to him with the suggestion of this big Dublin piece and he bought into it , ' ' said Walsh . <p> " Let 's see ... there are columns by John Healy and Eamonn McCann , some motoring at the back , a couple of editorials and the Wigmore column . <p> " The rest of the magazine is ' Return to the Hill ' - 15 interviews with Heffo 's players . I think it was 39,000 words in total . <p> " We all thought it would be good . These Dublin football players were such interesting people . They were an unusual collection in that their back stories were really interesting and what they had made of their lives . The reason why they were chosen by Heffernan was their physical prowess and their intellectual might . They were successful people in their careers . <p> " So we knew it was going to be interesting if they committed to it , which they players really did . Magill sold out in two days . You could n't get a copy . <p> " I had a guy staying in my house this week and he said that he always had a copy of that Magill in his flat but it was stolen . I 've heard that plenty of times . People enjoyed it , for sure . ' ' <p> Long before Lance Armstrong or his move to the UK to work for The Sunday Times , Walsh worked his way up the ranks in Irish sports journalism . <p> A Kilkenny native , a first posting with the Leitrim Observer was a leap into the unknown , but he loved his two years there . <p> " I 'd gone to UCD to study English and Geography and loved it there . It was the best time of my life , ' ' he said . <p> " I had the distinction of playing for the UCD seconds in soccer . There was a practise game where we took on the first . I was in midfield and my direct opponent was Kevin Moran . I was on the receiving end of a couple of his tackles . It was like being hit by a tank . <p> " I watched the rise of the Dublin team , and Kevin would become part of it , with a feeling of distaste , to be honest . Did n't want them to win - that 's the way culchies felt . But they ended up becoming such an impressive bunch of people . " <p> After his stint in Leitrim , Walsh went on to work for The Irish Press , The Sunday Tribune and The Sunday Independent , as well as Magill . <p> But nothing he wrote during his time in Ireland had as much impact as ' Return to the Hill ' . It is generallly regarded as the greatest piece of GAA journalism . Strangely , though , he never asked Heffernan himself to be part of it . <p> " It was because he intimidated me -- not on a personal level , but it was his aura . I felt that he would n't do it . It was unprofessional of me and I should have spoken to him , ' ' said Walsh . <p> " Maybe it was n't a bad thing , though , because the players felt freer knowing that it was just their stories and that Heffo was n't going to be an overseeing presence in the magazine . <p> " When I was interviewing John McCarthy and he uttered his famous line - ' If Heffernan was walking down the street , I 'd cross to the other side <p> rather than having to say hello to him ' - I think he said that knowing that it was the story of John himself and all the other players . <p> " I would have told them that I was n't interviewing Heffernan , just the players . And I think the players would have realised that this was the chance to tell it from their point of view . ' ' <p> Again and again , the players reference Heffo . He was the master builder , they were the clay he used as his raw material . <p> Walsh mined such gold from all his interviewees that it begs the question - was he never tempted to turn it into a book ? <p> " When you look at it now , you might say that , but it never struck me . It was 1989 and I was 35 years of age . It felt like I was a young and relatively inexperienced journalist . Full of enthusiasm , ' ' he said . <p> " I ended up having nine interviews to write up in the last three days before the deadline . I had no help with transcription . <p> " I wrote three interviews per day for three consecutive days . If I had to write one 3000 interview now , I would be looking for three days to write it . Honestly , that 's not a joke . ' ' <p> Walsh turns 70 next year and , though he might not be in the final straight of his career , he 's coming around the bend . <p> " I 'm still enjoying it but I certainly do n't have far to go . When you have a big story that you know is interesting and going to be difficult <p> and challenging , it is great , ' ' he said . <p> " Covering the common or garden stuff does n't appeal to me as much as it would have in the past . <p> " I 'll always remember an old linotype operator , Bernie , from my time in the Leitrim Observer . He was an institution in the newspaper , still working away well into his 70s . <p> " I 'll never forget something he said to me - ' once I could do it all night , now I spend all night trying to do it once ' . <p> " I feel that I have reached that stage in my journey in this life . " <h> IVY DAY IN THE COMMITTEE ROOM <p> IT 'S WORTH going back over some of the key messages put out by Gavin Jim on the day of his first public appearance as Dublin manager back in 2012 . <p> As ever , he looked the part . Polished shoes . Impeccably tailored navy suit , with a tie to match . A gleaming white shirt . Not a hair out of place . <p> But it was his words that mattered most . <p> " I would have seen the values of Dublin football always being to express yourself as a footballer in a controlled manner , ' ' he said . <p> " That 's what I would see as the heritage and the tradition of Gaelic football within Dublin , that there is a collective sense of purpose about them in a very hard-nosed way . <p> " I 'm very much a facilitator . I set a game-plan , set a framework but I want players to express themselves . <p> " If you 're beating a player with a stick , he 's not going to express himself . " <p> The day after was when his work really began . Meeting after meeting . It was October 6 - a date once known as Ivy Day , in honour of Charles Stewart Parnell 's passing . And a football revolution started in a ground named after him . <p> History matters to Gavin . Parnell died in 1891 - Dublin 's first All-Ireland was from that year . He once took his players to meet some of the surviving relatives of that team . <p> Gavin is a product of two worlds - Dublin GAA and the Irish Air Corps . <p> And his military training has had a huge bearing on his work in Gaelic football . <p> Gavin has always been a high achiever . He went into the Defence Forces at a time when only 30 cadets a year were chosen . And , of those 30 , only half-a-dozen made it into the Air Corps . <p> So much is made of Dublin 's - very real - advantages in terms of population , finance and familiarity with Croke Park , but there are things Gavin introduced that have been hugely effective that other counties could easily copy . <p> Gavin regularly talked up clubs and grassroots during his media briefings . And it was n't just lip service , either . <p> He introduced annual meetings where club managers and chairmen in the capital were invited to a meeting . The Dublin backroom team also got to share their expertise , and the aim was to strengthen club football in Dublin which , in turn , would strengthen the senior team . <p> Kieran Duff , an iconic Dub in the 1980s , feels Gavin and his players still do n't get enough credit for transforming football in the capital . <p> " After retiring , I became a fan again . I go to every game and what people seem to forget is that after 1977 , and up until 2011 , we won just two All-Irelands , ' ' he said . <p> " This team was special and it was special because it 's a team . This ability Jim had to constantly rebuild and win . Each year someone new came into the side , Brian Howard , Con O'Callaghan ... Play well , you keep your place . Slack off , you 're on the sideline . ' ' <p> Gavin walked away when the historic five-in-a-row was achieved . Now he 's diligently working on plans to make Gaelic football a more attractive sport . <p> Maybe what is most remarkable is that we know little about what makes one of the most high profile people in Irish sport tick . He 's still the man with the Mona Lisa smile . An enigma . <h> A PAINFUL CASE <p> FOLLOWING his election to Dublin City Council in June , Gavin Pepper posted a video of himself on social media wearing a Dublin jersey . <p> A couple of days later , CMAT took to the stage in Fairview Park , and put on one of the gigs of the year . <p> She did n't just wear a Dublin jersey , she had the shorts as well . <p> Pepper is an independent councillor who stood on an anti-immigration platform and was described by TD Paul Murphy , using D ? il privilege , as a far right activist . <p> CMAT is bisexual , an LGBT icon who pulled out of this summer 's Latitude festival in protest at sponsor Barclay Bank 's links to Israel . <p> Pepper and CMAT could not be more different , but the way both embrace the Dublin colours reflects an increasingly changing , conflicted and turbulent city . <p> In two recent documentaries - North Circular and The Locals - filmmaker Luke McManus put the focus on some of the most neglected parts of the capital . What is ' Dubness ' in 2024 ? <p> " That is hard to pin down , but I would think a dark humour , a mixture of pain and hilarity are key to it , ' ' he said . <p> " A melancholy nostalgia combined with a keen sense of narrative - there 's a reason Dubs greet each other with ' what 's the story ? ' <p> " They demand narrative from each other . Also a mild confusion about their own identity - someone called Dublin a city between Ireland and England and I think there 's truth to that . As a Corkman told me once : ' Ye are all practically fucking Brits ' . ' ' <p> When Dublin won a first All-Ireland in 12 years in 1995 , it was a particularly special time for Balbriggan . <p> Captain John O'Leary hailed from there , starting his football journey with O'Dwyers . <p> Nearly three decades on , Balbriggan is a very different place , something documented to great effect by Kevin Curran in his novel ' Youth ' . <p> Balbriggan is Ireland 's most diverse town and ' Youth ' focuses on teenagers with African heritage . <p> Curran 's day job is as a teacher in the town and he does n't feel the Dublin football team really resonates with many of his students . <p> " There has been a marked rise in the number of young lads who have started to support League of Ireland teams , ' ' he said . <p> " As far as GAA goes , in our school , there would n't be a massive Dublin GAA following . When we put out Gaelic teams in our school , maybe one third would actually play Gaelic , the rest would all be football players . <p> " Recently we played a final game against a team from Blanch , and you 'd think the make up of the teams would be similar . <p> " All of the players on the Blanch teams were white , and all played Gaelic . But around half of our starting team were black and none of them played Gaelic for a club . We won the final . <p> " Even when GAA scouts at the final were looking and talking to some of our players and asking them to join a GAA club , the lads were more interested in DDSL soccer than GAA . <p> " We 've been talking to some lads telling them about the opportunities they could get in college etc if they played Gaelic , but they 're not interested . ' ' <p> It should be noted that , since Jason Sherlock became the first mixed-race Dubliner to win an All-Ireland in 1995 , only one other has done so - Craig Dias . <p> Paul Page played guitar with Whipping Boy , one of the best Irish bands of the 1990s . <p> He was an inner city kid , growing up on Dominick Street . He hears bands now - like A Lazarus Soul and Lankum and Gurriers - singing in their own strong Dublin accents , and marvels at the shift . <p> " They are singing in their own voice , their authentic voice and they are not afraid to hide that . When I was growing up , there was probably a stigma attached to being from the inner city , ' ' he said . <p> " It had a really bad reputation as a heroin black-spot , but there are so many good people in those communities . Some of the most generous people and open-hearted people I 've met came from the likes of Dominick Street and other communities like Dorset Street . <p> " When we were in school , we were told if we were applying for a job , do n't use your own address . So it 's great to see that confidence there . <p> " Going back to when I was a kid , I remember the lift the city got from Heffo 's team . Sport and music have that capacity to lift collective spirits and engender a kind of sense of pride in the place you 're from . ' ' <p> But there 's a delicate balancing act . An excess of pride can lead to exceptionalism , and Page is concerned at the rise of anti-immigration sentiment . <p> " I definitely think we are seeing that , even the way the far right have adopted the Irish flag as some kind of symbol . That they are somehow more patriotic than the rest of us , ' ' he said . <p> " I just think that is really dangerous territory . I think , by and large , Dublin 's a better place now than it was when I was growing up . I think the multicultural element is something that 's a real positive . I accept some people feel threatened by it but I do n't see it as a negative myself and I do feel the confidence of the city has been knocked by the riots last November . " <p> Irish sport - at international level , especially - has benefited greatly from the contribution of migrants , and the descendents of migrants . <p> But sportspeople have been targeted for abuse because of their skin colour . <p> " Just look at the reaction about a year ago to the Ireland Under-15 football team doing really well at some tournament . I think the team was maybe 50 or 70 per cent black , ' ' said Curran . <p> " The comments and reaction was a disgrace . The team itself was a reflection of teams we would put out for our school teams , so it was no shock to us , but mainstream Ireland was n't ready to see that kind of team wearing the Ireland jersey and they reacted badly to it . " <p> In another 50 years , what will a Dub be ? One thing 's for sure , it 's an ever changing idea . <p> " I think that it 's a reasonably open identity . But you have to work pretty hard at it and so some may falter on their way , ' ' said McManus . <p> " In a city that runs on talk , that is stacked with great conversational practitioners , it can be hard to speak up and make an impact if English is n't your first language . <p> " And there are plenty of Dubs who want to pull up the ladder too , unfortunately . But if it is a state of mind , that means it is open to all who want it and are willing to put in the hard yards . ' ' <h> A MOTHER <p> IT WAS the highlight of many people 's weeks . Thursday evening meant Top of the Pops . <p> But the audience in Ireland for one particular edition in November , 1878 , went through the roof . <p> That 's because The Boomtown Rats were at number one with ' Rat Trap ' . <p> The old footage of their appearance tells us how much had changed in the capital , with drummer Simon Crowe wearing an ' Up the Dubs ' headband . <p> Heffernan had moulded a team that even had cachet with ' 70s rockers . <p> The lyrics of ' Rat Trap ' mention the Five Lamps , and that north inner city landmark is familiar to many from their treks to Croke Park . <p> There is a charity shop opposite it and it has a track record of unearthing gems . During the summer , an official poster marking Dublin 's millennium in 1988 was in the window . <p> It depicted famous Dubliners down the years . U2 were there and Brendan Behan . So were Stephen Roche , Brendan Behan and James Joyce . In total , 17 people were included - all of them men . <p> That chimed with the GAA story too . Women were there as support and to be thanked at AGMs for making the tea and sandwiches . That was about it . <p> A great sea change happened , and Julie Kavanagh was a big part of it . 1988 was a special year for her too because it was when she started playing football with St Monica 's . <p> Just a year later , she would pick up the player of the match award after Dublin beat Clare in a replay of the All-Ireland junior final . <p> Dublin have won six senior All-Irelands since 2010 , contesting another four finals in that time . <p> But it all began for the Jackies with that junior win in 1989 . Three years later , Kavanagh would be an All Star , the first outfield player from the capital to win one . <p> " There were no prominent Dublin female footballers to look up to when I was growing up . We would have looked up to the likes of Paul Curran and Keith Barr , ' ' she said . <p> " From travelling around the country , you got to know other players and teams . Mary Jo Curran of Kerry would have been a role model for me , and to play against her was great . ' ' <p> Kavanagh believes there is no great secret behind Dublin 's rise in ladies football . Hard work was the key . <p> " Pathways were put in place , structures were improved , and different divisions to cater for young girls came into being , ' ' she said . <p> " Development squads came into play too . I think I was involved in one of the first Dublin development squads . <p> " I have daughters of 21 and 18 playing adult football now and , when they were coming through , they had F ? ile , they were playing at the right age-group all along . Compare that to my time , when we were playing senior football at 14 or 15 . " <p> It is noteworthy that , at sponsorship launches , Dublin invariably have players from ladies football and camogie as well as from Gaelic football and hurling . No other county does this , and it is a positive , according to Kavanagh . <p> " It is something I did notice with Dublin . I think it 's a good thing . It gives the recognition to the girls as well as the lads . <p> " When we started playing , we were lucky if we got a sponsor . There was a time we were training in the Phoenix Park and we had four footballs , we were blessed to have four footballs . It has come a long way . <p> " We came through football at a time when we struggled to get things . We were outside Croke Park on men 's All-Ireland final day shaking buckets . That money probably went towards a bus to bring us to a first Championship match . It 's great that things have changed . " <h> GRACE <p> THE MUSIC was ramping up , the crowd were buzzing , there was electricity in the Dublin air . Diamond Park in the north inner city was packed to the gills . <p> A big screen had been erected to show Kellie Harrington 's Olympic final in Paris and it was the hottest - free - ticket in town . Many of those inside were family , neighbours and friends of Harrington . She started boxing at Corinthians on Bella Street . <p> The founder of the club stood at one side of the park , eyes glued to the big screen . With his tan and thick salt and pepper hair , he looked like a successful middle-aged businessman <p> It was Gerry Hutch , a man with a track record as the leader of an organised crime gang . <p> The Hutch gang 's feud with the Kinahan cartel cost the lives of 18 people . Many of those were killed in the streets around Diamond Park . <p> When Harrington 's hand was raised in victory on August 6 , the Park exploded . The party would go on all night in houses around her native Portland Row . <p> In Paris , the boxer would mark her second Olympic gold with a rendition of ' Grace ' , a song of the 1916 Rising . <h> THE DEAD <p> HE 'D celebrated his 85th birthday just a month earlier , but Mickey Whelan was full of youthful fire and energy that day in Belfast . <p> There was care in the way he 'd dressed . He took to the stage outside City Hall wearing a sky-blue shirt and a Palestine scarf . <p> And , when he spoke into the mic , he gave a stirring and passionate speech calling for a ceasefire in Gaza . At one stage , Whelan reached for the words of William Wilberforce - " You may choose to look away , but you can not say that you did not know . " <p> Wilberforce was an English MP who led the campaign to abolish slavery in the 18th century . <p> Whelan was Kevin Heffernan 's sounding post in the 1970s , he was Pat Gilroy 's coach when Dublin lifted Sam in 2011 , he is still working alongside him with the St Vincent 's hurlers . Need we remind you he 's 85 . <p> The scarf was a gift from David Hickey , and the latter often references the Dublin team that inspired him . <p> " For me , getting on the Dublin team was the dream . My father was from Cork and my mother was from Limerick . We spent every summer growing up down on a farm in Limerick , ' ' he said . <p> " But seeing the Dublin team in the early 1960s -- Mickey Whelan , Des Foley , Laurence Foley - my only ambition in life was to play football for Dublin . That was my identity with Dublin , the football team . <p> " Now I am far more aware of what Dublin is and Dublin is n't . Brendan Behan , Luke Kelly , The Dubliners , James Joyce -- we have a great legacy of culture and literature in the city . I am more Dublin now than I was . " <p> What is Dublin ? Its people , and its people come from all over now . <p> Back in April , the Stardust relatives marked victory in their long campaign for justice by singing ' You 'll Never Walk Alone ' on a parade to the Garden of Remembrance . The Dublin fans sang the same soccer hymn when Sean Doherty raised Sam Maguire in 1974 . <p> Dublin football was never detached from its people , the connections were everywhere . <p> When St Vincent 's won the All-Ireland club title a decade ago , Kevin Bonnie was at corner-back . His mother was one of the Dunne 's Store strikers who , 40 years ago , effectively changed the Irish government 's stance towards apartheid South Africa . <p> Many of those who go to cheer on the Dubs in Croke Park stop to get photographs taken at the murals in Ballybough . One depicts four of the men of 1974 - Jimmy Keaveney and the now departed Heffo , Brian Mullins and Anton O'Toole . The other is in honour of Jim Gavin . <p> They are the work of an artist from Wicklow , Ruairi O'Byrne . " Growing up , we did n't have a great history of going to Croke Park . GAA was always outside my experience . It was n't something that I was involved in down in Wicklow , ' ' he said . <p> " It 's mad because when I go up to add more to it , I start talking to the locals there . It 's quite a social thing and it 's such a lovely place to paint around there . <p> " It 's such a close-knit community that you really feel that you are part of something . Any time I drive through Dublin , I see the murals , realise that they are still there and how well they are looked after . You realise how important they are in terms of what they bring to the supporters . " <p> Capturing something of the essence of Dublin icons was a challenge for O'Byrne , and he knew he had to get it right . <p> " I 'd look at photographs and video clips . You see them playing , what dramatic shots would work in the space available on the mural , ' ' he said . <p> " We had Brian Mullins running down the field , volleying the ball . We had to make sure that it will fit in with Anton O'Toole 's picture also . <p> " We wanted to pick shots that worked within the frame of the mural but not take too much emphasis away from it , as it is a team . We also wanted to leave space for any future memorials . " <p> A reminder of the great wheel of life . Of games and footballers and heroes to come . <h> AFTERWORD <p> IT 'S a handsome notebook . More than a decade old , but well preserved . If there was a Dublin GAA museum , Jim Gavin 's notebooks would draw the crowds . <p> He has one of them on the table before him , dating back to the time before he even got the Dublin manager 's job . Gavin finds the pages he 's looking for , and the detail is a little window into how the most successful manager in GAA history operated . <p> He is talking about the 1970s Dublin team that he reveres , and how he studied the video footage of Stephen Rooney , a player who he rates but is often overlooked . <p> When Gavin started coaching , he dug into Dublin history , looking to learn from the past . <p> " I knew you were coming in so I was looking back on an old notebook that I had on the 1974 team . Go back 20 or 30 years before that . They were predominantly beaten in Leinster semi-finals or the first round . On to 1957 , they get to a Leinster final , win the All-Ireland the following year , ' ' said Gavin . <p> " Lose to Kerry the following year and then back to the cycle of losing in the first round in Leinster . Get to the All-Ireland final in 1962 , lose to Kerry , win the following year and then back to the cycle of losing to Meath , Kildare , Westmeath , Longford in Leinster . <p> " It 's not going well for Dublin . Kevin Heffernan came in for the League of 1973/74 and they did really well in it . They were in Division 2A with the likes of Clare , Wexford and Limerick . <p> " That caught my eye - Dublin 's success in the ' 70s was predominantly based on good League campaigns . My attitude was that you try and win every competition that you 're in . Forgive me , but there 's a process - you 're probably going ' Jaysus , here he goes again ' - but , if you get your performance bit right , you 'll hopefully win . You want to do your best and try and win the bloody thing . Kevin must have had a similar outlook because they kept getting into League finals . " <p> Before we sat down , Gavin conducted a tour of his workplace and it 's clear that he loves the place . It chimes with his sense of history , that 's for sure . <p> Gavin is Chief Operations Officer with the Irish Aviation Authority , who are based on D'Olier Street in Dublin . <p> It 's a building that used to house The Irish Times . That was n't the first newspaper to have an office there . <p> A plaque on the front is dedicated to the memory of Easter Rising leader Se ? n Mac Diarmada . He edited the monthly newspaper ' Irish Freedom ' from a tiny upstairs office . <p> History is on Gavin 's mind as Sunday marks 50 years since the most important day in the history of Dublin football - the breakthrough All-Ireland win for Heffernan 's team against Galway . Nothing would ever be the same again . <p> " I was born in 1971 , my father , Jimmy , went to a lot of the games - he passed away last year - and he used to bring back the programmes from the All-Ireland finals . What I remember as a kid growing up is the colour , the colour of the programmes , the colour of the Dublin jerseys , the Kerry jerseys , the Armagh jerseys on TV , ' ' said Gavin . <p> " That 's one thing that sticks in my mind . The crepe hat that my father brought back , with the Dublin colours . My Dad would have been my first coach , and he 'd talk often of the likes of Jimmy Keaveney , Anton O'Toole and Bobby Doyle . <p> " These were names that were referenced at home over dinner - ' did you watch so-and-so ? ' The games would be talked about at home and , then in the back garden , you 'd try and be Tony Hanahoe or John McCarthy or whoever . But it 's the colour that sticks in my mind the most . ' ' <p> The colours matter . Gavin is sporting a sky blue pocket square when we meet . He does n't do anything by accident . <p> It 's largely forgotten now that Gavin was actually turned down the first time he went for the Dublin manager 's job . <p> With the benefit of hindsight , he feels one of the best things to happen to him was Heffo saying no . <p> " When Paul Caffrey stepped down , I had one year done with the 21s and I was approached to see if I would consider putting my name forward , ' ' he said . <p> " In the room when I walked in for the interview were John Costello , Dr Pat O'Neill , Robbie Kelleher and Kevin Heffernan . <p> " We had a really deep conversation about football and Kevin was very curious about how I wanted Dublin to play , not so much about winning . <p> " Thankfully , he gave the job to Pat Gilroy because , if I got it then , I 'd probably have been out the door after six months . <p> " I was n't ready . I made mistakes when I was senior manager but I made a hell of a lot more when I was managing the 21s , so I need to thank Kevin for seeing that in me , genuinely . He saw that I was n't ready . <p> " Pat did a phenomenal job . I always slag Pat that I left my notes behind me - which I did . My folder with all my philosophy of football . " <p> So you should have be credited with an extra All-Ireland ? Gavin laughs in response . <p> Paddy Cullen and Ger Brennan are the only players from between the canals to play Championship football for Dublin in the past 60 years . <p> Given the fact that the population of the area is close to 120,000 , it 's clear that the GAA has a major issue there . <p> " The root cause is political and planning incompetence . My Mum and Dad grew up in west Clare , moved to Clondalkin and there were green fields on the banks of the Grand Canal , ' ' said Gavin . <p> " Due to corruption , I imagine , estate upon estate were built with very few green spaces , with very few amenities . That 's just incompetence . <p> " I 've looked at the maps of the inner city and there was plenty of space back in the day for green field sites . From poor planning and incompetent planning , you have what you have . <p> " It 's not just one sporting organisation that have issues there , it 's them all . There used to be land at the back of Croke Park . It 's now a car park . It was a football field back in the day , it 's all concrete now . <p> " There were opportunities down the years and leaders then did n't take them . What we 're left with is concrete everywhere . There were clubs in the inner city but they 've all moved out to the suburbs because they needed land . <p> In May of last year , Jim Gavin was announced as the chair of the North East Inner City ( NEIC ) Implementation Board . <p> " It 's that area of Dublin centred on the Five Lamps . Five areas come together there - Ballybough , North Strand , Summerhill , Sheriff Street and Amiens Street , ' ' he said . <p> " It 's an area where there was a lot of gangland crime and murders and a big drugs problem . Kieran Mulvey was put in charge of a task force by the government , lifted the lid and sees there 's a lot more than drugs and murders - issues with everything from health to education to employment . <p> " From there , the government set up the North East Inner City initiative . There 's a programme of things that need to be done from safety and security to community well being to the physical landscape of the area . <p> " I walk around the area every second day on my lunch break and there 's a sense of safety . Is it where it should be ? Absolutely not , there 's still a long way to go but the Guards are doing a really good job . They need more resources , we all know that . <p> " The tide is definitely turning . It does n't help when people from outside the community come in and cause chaos . We saw that with the riots last year . ' ' <p> Anyone who visits Dublin will be quickly aware that it is a typically multicultural European city . <p> That brings challenges , and getting migrants - and their children - interested in the GAA has been a slow process . <p> Some of the biggest stars in Irish sport - like Gavin Bazunu and Rhasidat Adeleke - are from migrant backgrounds . <p> But Jason Sherlock and Craig Dias are the only players from a mixed race background to play senior Championship football for Dublin . <p> Gavin accepts that this is an area where a lot more work is needed , and he feels it 's not a new problem , either . <p> " Back in 1995 , Paul Curran was one of the first GPOs ( Games Promotion Officers ) and he brought myself and Jason Sherlock with Sam Maguire to a school in Ronanstown , ' ' he said . <p> " Some of the kids did n't know what the cup was but they recognised Jason , because of the glamour of Jason Sherlock - he wo n't want me saying that . He was the poster boy of Gaelic football back then . They did n't have a clue who I was , or Paul - and Paul had been man of the match in the All-Ireland final . <p> " There are tracts of land in the city that have become very difficult for the GAA to penetrate , there 's that side of it . <p> " The other side are the New Irish , shall we say , and getting them . With the 21s , I had Luke Sweetman , Ted Furman and Lorcan Siwale . I 've seen some come through but could we be doing more ? Absolutely . There 's a lot to be said for putting more effort in , that 's for sure . ' ' <p> The mural artist Ruairi O'Byrne was commissioned to paint two Dublin murals in Ballybough - one honours Heffo and his men , the other is of Gavin to mark the historic five-in-a-row . <p> " I 've seen it , of course , and it 's a strange experience to see a mural of yourself . Thankfully , it has n't been damaged . Nobody has drawn a moustache on me ! <p> " The mural ... I see it as a representation of the guys and , ultimately , Dublin GAA <p> " It 's close to Croke Park and even though there is n't a GAA club in the area , they 're all very proud of Dublin GAA . They might n't be affiliated to GAA clubs but they 're passionate clubs . Dublin flags everywhere . ' ' <p> Just a couple of months into Gavin 's tenure as Dublin manager , he found himself at Heffernan 's funeral . It was a day that made clear to him the status and responsibility that comes with managing Dublin . <p> " Kevin passed away early in 2013 , the start of my first year . I remember going to the church in Marino for the funeral and I was told to go up to the front pews because I was the Dublin manager . I was thinking ' God , this is the role I have now ' . ' ' <p> That role would see him taking Dublin to unprecedented heights , but Gavin was always conscious of the past informing the present . <p> " We had a reunion before Christmas last year of the 2013 team , but the 1983 and 1963 Dublin All-Ireland winning sides were part of it too , ' ' he said . <p> Pascal Flynn , goalkeeper on the ' 63 team , passed away this week . Along with Heffernan , O'Toole and Brian Mullins are no longer with us from the 1970s , but the blue flame still burns . Eternally . <p> Story Saved <p> You can find this story in My Bookmarks.Or by navigating to the user icon in the top right .
